Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\n\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. \"---\\ntitle: Mark Williams (snooker player) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-04-23\\n---\\nBorn\\t21 March 1975 (age 50)[1]: 184 \\nCwm, Wales\\nSport country\\t Wales\\nNickname\\tThe Welsh Potting Machine[2]\\nProfessional\\t1992–present\\nHighest ranking\\t1 (May 2000 – May 2002,\\nMay 2003 – May 2004,\\nMay–September 2011)\\nCurrent ranking\\t3 (as of 5 May 2025)\\nMaximum breaks\\t3\\nCentury breaks\\t668 (as of 5 July 2025)\\n\\nMark James Williams (born 21March 1975) is a Welsh professional snooker player who is a three-time World Champion, winning the title in 2000, 2003 and 2018. He has been ranked the world number one player three times (May 2000 May 2002, May 2003 May 2004 and May 2011 September 2011). His most successful season to date was 200203, when he won snooker's Triple Crown—the UK Championship, the Masters and the World Championship—making him only the third player, after Steve Davis and Stephen Hendry, to have won all three events in the same season. He is the first, and to date, the only player to win all three versions of the professional world championship: the World Snooker Championship, the Six-red World Championship and the World Seniors Championship.\\n\\nWilliams became a professional player in 1992. He has won 26 ranking tournaments, including two UK Championships (1999 and 2002), placing him sixth on the all-time list of ranking titles. He has also won the Masters tournament on two occasions (1998 and 2003). His form began to decline after his second World Championship title in 2003; he then dropped out of the top 16 following the 200708 season but regained his place for 200910. After winning the 2011 \", \"total_chars\": 117204, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"38231\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ding_Junhui\", \"score\": 0.6132797598838806, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Ding Junhui - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-02-06\\n---\\nname: Ding Junhui\\nbirth_date: 1 April 1987\\nbirth_place: Yixing, Jiangsu, China\\nprofessional: 2003–present\\nhigh ranking: 1 (December 2014, January–February 2015)\\nofficial maximums: 7\\nranking wins: 15\\nminor wins: 4\\nmedals-expand: no\\nmedals: World Games, 2005 Duisburg, Asian Games, 2002 Busan Individual, 2002 Busan Team, 2006 Doha Individual, 2006 Doha Doubles, 2006 Doha Team, 2010 Guangzhou Individual, 2010 Guangzhou Team, Asian Indoor and Martial Arts Games, 2013 Incheon Individual, 2013 Incheon Team\\n\\ns: 丁俊晖\\nt: 丁俊暉\\np: Dīng Jùnhuī\\nj: Ding¹ Zeon³ Fai¹\\ny: Dīng Jeun Fāi\\nmi: ing un ui\\norder: st\\n\\nDing Junhui (; born 1 April 1987) is a Chinese professional snooker player. He is the most successful Asian player in the history of the sport. Throughout his career, he has won 15 major ranking titles, including three UK Championships (2005, 2009, 2019), and in 2014, became the first Asian world number one. He has twice reached the final of the Masters, winning once in 2011. In 2016, he became the first Asian player to reach the final of the World Championship.\\n\\nDing began playing snooker at age nine and rose to international prominence in 2002 after winning the Asian Under-21 Championship and the Asian Championship. At age 15, he became the youngest winner of the IBSF World Under-21 Championship. In 2003, Ding turned professional at the age of 16. His first major professional successes came in 2005 when he won the China Open and the UK Championship, becoming the first player from outside Great Britain and Ireland to win the title.\\n\", \"total_chars\": 75871, \"truncated\": true}]"

Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored the Last 147 in Snooker?\\n\\nWith Noppon Saengkham scoring the firs\", \"total_chars\": 48505, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.6863283514976501, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Masters_(snooker)\", \"score\": 0.6234679222106934, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: 2022 Masters (snooker) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2021-04-15\\n---\\ntournament_name: 2022 Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. \"---\\ntitle: Mark Williams (snooker player) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-04-23\\n---\\nBorn\\t21 March 1975 (age 50)[1]: 184 \\nCwm, Wales\\nSport country\\t Wales\\nNickname\\tThe Welsh Potting breaks\\t3\\nCentury breaks\\t668 (as of 5 July 2025)\\n\\nMark James Williams (born 21March 1975) is a Welsh professional snooker player who is a three-time World Champion, winning the title in 2000, 2003 and 2018. He has been ranked the world number one player three times (May 2000 May 2002, May 2003 May 2004 and May 2011 September 2011). His most successful season to date was 200203, when he won snooker's Triple Crown—the UK Championship, the Masters and the World Championship—making him only the third player, after Steve Davis and Stephen Hendry, to have won all three events in the same season. He is the first, and to date, the only player to win all three versions of the professional world championship: the World Snooker Championship, the Six-red World Championship and the World Seniors Championship.\\n\\nWilliams became a professional player in 1992. He has won 26 ranking tournaments, including two UK Championships (1999 and 2002), placing him sixth on the all-time list of ranking titles. He has also won the Masters tournament on two occasions (1998 and 2003). His form began to decline after his second World Championship title in 2003; he then dropped out of the top 16 following the 200708 season but regained his place for 200910. After winning the 2011 \", \"total_chars\": 117204, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"38231\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ding_Junhui\", \"score\": 0.6132797598838806, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Ding Junhui - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-02-06\\n---\\nname: Ding Junhui\\nbirth_date: 1 April 1987\\nbirth_place: Yixing, Jiangsu, China\\nprofessional: 2003–present\\nhigh ranking: 1 (December 2014, January–February 2015)\\nofficial maximums: 7\\nranking wins: 15\\nminor wins: 4\\nmedals-expand: no\\nmedals: World Games, 2005 Duisburg, Asian Games, 2002 Busan Individual, 2002 Busan Team, 2006 Doha Individual, 2006 Doha Doubles, 2006 Doha Team, 2010 Guangzhou Individual, 2010 Guangzhou Team, Asian Indoor and Martial Arts Games, 2013 Incheon Individual, 2013 Incheon Team\\n\\ns: 丁俊晖\\nt: 丁俊暉\\np: Dīng Jùnhuī\\nj: Ding¹ Zeon³ Fai¹\\ny: Dīng Jeun Fāi\\nmi: ing un ui\\norder: st\\n\\nDing Junhui (; born 1 April 1987) is a Chinese professional snooker player. He is the most successful Asian player in the history of the sport. Throughout his career, he has won 15 major ranking titles, including three UK Championships (2005, 2009, 2019), and in 2014, became the first Asian world number one. He has twice reached the final of the Masters, winning once in 2011. In 2016, he became the first Asian player to reach the final of the World Championship.\\n\\nDing began playing snooker at age nine and rose to international prominence in 2002 after winning the Asian Under-21 Championship and the Asian Championship. At age 15, he became the youngest winner of the IBSF World Under-21 Championship. In 2003, Ding turned professional at the age of 16. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored the Last 147 in Snooker?\\n\\nWith Noppon Saengkham scoring the firs\", \"total_chars\": 48505, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.6816297769546509, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. \"---\\ntitle: Mark Williams (snooker player) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-04-23\\n---\\nBorn\\t21 March 1975 (age 50)[1]: 184 \\nCwm, Wales\\nSport country\\t Wales\\nNickname\\tThe Welsh Potting breaks\\t3\\nCentury breaks\\t668 (as of 5 July 2025)\\n\\nMark James Williams (born 21March 1975) is a Welsh professional snooker player who is a three-time World Champion, winning the title in 2000, 2003 and 2018. He has been ranked the world number one player three times (May 2000 May 2002, May 2003 May 2004 and May 2011 September 2011). His most successful season to date was 200203, when he won snooker's Triple Crown—the UK Championship, the Masters and the World Championship—making him only the third player, after Steve Davis and Stephen Hendry, to have won all three events in the same season. He is the first, and to date, the only player to win all three versions of the professional world championship: the World Snooker Championship, the Six-red World Championship and the World Seniors Championship.\\n\\nWilliams became a professional player in 1992. He has won 26 ranking tournaments, including two UK Championships (1999 and 2002), placing him sixth on the all-time list of ranking titles. He has also won the Masters tournament on two occasions (1998 and 2003). His form began to decline after his second World Championship title in 2003; he then dropped out of the top 16 following the 200708 season but regained his place for 200910. After winning the 2011 \", \"total_chars\": 117204, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"38231\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ding_Junhui\", \"score\": 0.6132797598838806, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Ding Junhui - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-02-06\\n---\\nname: Ding Junhui\\nbirth_date: 1 April 1987\\nbirth_place: Yixing, Jiangsu, China\\nprofessional: 2003–present\\nhigh ranking: 1 (December 2014, January–February 2015)\\nofficial maximums: 7\\nranking wins: 15\\nminor wins: 4\\nmedals-expand: no\\nmedals: World Games, 2005 Duisburg, Asian Games, 2002 Busan Individual, 2002 Busan Team, 2006 Doha Individual, 2006 Doha Doubles, 2006 Doha Team, 2010 Guangzhou Individual, 2010 Guangzhou Team, Asian Indoor and Martial Arts Games, 2013 Incheon Individual, 2013 Incheon Team\\n\\ns: 丁俊晖\\nt: 丁俊暉\\np: Dīng Jùnhuī\\nj: Ding¹ Zeon³ Fai¹\\ny: Dīng Jeun Fāi\\nmi: ing un ui\\norder: st\\n\\nDing Junhui (; born 1 April 1987) is a Chinese professional snooker player. He is the most successful Asian player in the history of the sport. Throughout his career, he has won 15 major ranking titles, including three UK Championships (2005, 2009, 2019), and in 2014, became the first Asian world number one. He has twice reached the final of the Masters, winning once in 2011. In 2016, he became the first Asian player to reach the final of the World Championship.\\n\\nDing began playing snooker at age nine and rose to international prominence in 2002 after winning the Asian Under-21 Championship and the Asian Championship. At age 15, he became the youngest winner of the IBSF World Under-21 Championship. In 2003, Ding turned professional at the age of 16.         "content": "[{\"docid\": \"64519\", \"url\": \"https://www.olbg.com/blogs/snooker-maximum-breaks\", \"score\": 0.6993581652641296, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: The Evolution of 147 Breaks in Snooker: From Rarity to Record-Breaking Feats\\nauthor: Author; Contributor Information\\ndate: 2023-08-17\\n---\\nEditor-In-Chief with 20 years experience covering the betting angles to breaking news stories. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored the Last 147 in Snooker?\\n\\nWith Noppon Saengkham scoring the firs\", \"total_chars\": 48505, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.6863283514976501, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Masters_(snooker)\", \"score\": 0.6234679222106934, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: 2022 Masters (snooker) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2021-04-15\\n---\\ntournament_name: 2022 Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt.         "content": "[{\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.7045632004737854, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. A similar \", \"total_chars\": 15184, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"64519\", \"url\": \"https://www.olbg.com/blogs/snooker-maximum-breaks\", \"score\": 0.6986712217330933, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: The Evolution of 147 Breaks in Snooker: From Rarity to Record-Breaking Feats\\nauthor: Author; Contributor Information\\ndate: 2023-08-17\\n---\\nEditor-In-Chief with 20 years experience covering the betting angles to breaking news stories. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. \"---\\ntitle: Mark Williams (snooker player) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-04-23\\n---\\nBorn\\t21 March 1975 (age 50)[1]: 184 \\nCwm, Wales\\nSport country\\t Wales\\nNickname\\tThe Welsh Potting breaks\\t3\\nCentury breaks\\t668 (as of 5 July 2025)\\n\\nMark James Williams (born 21March 1975) is a Welsh professional snooker player who is a three-time World Champion, winning the title in 2000, 2003 and 2018. He has been ranked the world number one player three times (May 2000 May 2002, May 2003 May 2004 and May 2011 September 2011). His most successful season to date was 200203, when he won snooker's Triple Crown—the UK Championship, the Masters and the World Championship—making him only the third player, after Steve Davis and Stephen Hendry, to have won all three events in the same season. He is the first, and to date, the only player to win all three versions of the professional world championship: the World Snooker Championship, the Six-red World Championship and the World Seniors Championship.\\n\\nWilliams became a professional player in 1992. He has won 26 ranking tournaments, including two UK Championships (1999 and 2002), placing him sixth on the all-time list of ranking titles. He has also won the Masters tournament on two occasions (1998 and 2003). His form began to decline after his second World Championship title in 2003; he then dropped out of the top 16 following the 200708 season but regained his place for 200910. After winning the 2011 \", \"total_chars\": 117204, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"38231\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ding_Junhui\", \"score\": 0.6132797598838806, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Ding Junhui - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-02-06\\n---\\nname: Ding Junhui\\nbirth_date: 1 April 1987\\nbirth_place: Yixing, Jiangsu, China\\nprofessional: 2003–present\\nhigh ranking: 1 (December 2014, January–February 2015)\\nofficial maximums: 7\\nranking wins: 15\\nminor wins: 4\\nmedals-expand: no\\nmedals: World Games, 2005 Duisburg, Asian Games, 2002 Busan Individual, 2002 Busan Team, 2006 Doha Individual, 2006 Doha Doubles, 2006 Doha Team, 2010 Guangzhou Individual, 2010 Guangzhou Team, Asian Indoor and Martial Arts Games, 2013 Incheon Individual, 2013 Incheon Team\\n\\ns: 丁俊晖\\nt: 丁俊暉\\np: Dīng Jùnhuī\\nj: Ding¹ Zeon³ Fai¹\\ny: Dīng Jeun Fāi\\nmi: ing un ui\\norder: st\\n\\nDing Junhui (; born 1 April 1987) is a Chinese professional snooker player. He is the most successful Asian player in the history of the sport. Throughout his career, he has won 15 major ranking titles, including three UK Championships (2005, 2009, 2019), and in 2014, became the first Asian world number one. He has twice reached the final of the Masters, winning once in 2011. In 2016, he became the first Asian player to reach the final of the World Championship.\\n\\nDing began playing snooker at age nine and rose to international prominence in 2002 after winning the Asian Under-21 Championship and the Asian Championship. At age 15, he became the youngest winner of the IBSF World Under-21 Championship. In 2003, Ding turned professional at the age of 16. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored the Last 147 in Snooker?\\n\\nWith Noppon Saengkham scoring the firs\", \"total_chars\": 48505, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.6863283514976501, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. A similar \", \"total_chars\": 15184, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"64519\", \"url\": \"https://www.olbg.com/blogs/snooker-maximum-breaks\", \"score\": 0.6986712217330933, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: The Evolution of 147 Breaks in Snooker: From Rarity to Record-Breaking Feats\\nauthor: Author; Contributor Information\\ndate: 2023-08-17\\n---\\nEditor-In-Chief with 20 years experience covering the betting angles to breaking news stories. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. Three maximum breaks were made during the event, compiled by Jak Jones in Group 2, David Gilbert in Group 7 and Mark Selby in the Winners' Group; only the fourth time that three maximums had been made in a tournament, after the 2012 UK Championship, the 2017 German Masters, and the previo\", \"total_chars\": 18835, \"truncated\": true}]"

This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. \"---\\ntitle: Mark Williams (snooker player) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2004-04-23\\n---\\nBorn\\t21 March 1975 (age 50)[1]: 184 \\nCwm, Wales\\nSport country\\t Wales\\nNickname\\tThe Welsh Potting breaks\\t3\\nCentury breaks\\t668 (as of 5 July 2025)\\n\\nMark James Williams (born 21March 1975) is a Welsh professional snooker player who is a three-time World Champion, winning the title in 2000, 2003 and 2018. He has been ranked the world number one player three times (May 2000 May 2002, May 2003 May 2004 and May 2011 September 2011). His most successful season to date was 200203, when he won snooker's Triple Crown—the UK Championship, the Masters and the World Championship—making him only the third player, after Steve Davis and Stephen Hendry, to have won all three events in the same season. He is the first, and to date, the only player to win all three versions of the professional world championship: the World Snooker Championship, the Six-red World Championship and the World Seniors Championship.\\n\\nWilliams became a professional player in 1992. He has won 26 ranking tournaments, including two UK Championships (1999 and 2002), placing him sixth on the all-time list of ranking titles. He has also won the Masters tournament on two occasions (1998 and 2003). His form began to decline after his second World Championship title in 2003; he then dropped out of the top 16 following the 200708 season but regained his place for 200910. He is the most successful Asian player in the history of the sport. Throughout his career, he has won 15 major ranking titles, including three UK Championships (2005, 2009, 2019), and in 2014, became the first Asian world number one. He has twice reached the final of the Masters, winning once in 2011. In 2016, he became the first Asian player to reach the final of the World Championship.\\n\\nDing began playing snooker at age nine and rose to international prominence in 2002 after winning the Asian Under-21 Championship and the Asian Championship. At age 15, he became the youngest winner of the IBSF World Under-21 Championship. In 2003, Ding turned professional at the age of 16. Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. Three maximum breaks were made during the event, compiled by Jak Jones in Group 2, David Gilbert in Group 7 and Mark Selby in the Winners' Group; only the fourth time that three maximums had been made in a tournament, after the 2012 UK Championship, the 2017 German Masters, and the previo\", \"total_chars\": 18835, \"truncated\": true}]"

        "content": "[{\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.7045632004737854, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. A similar \", \"total_chars\": 15184, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"64519\", \"url\": \"https://www.olbg.com/blogs/snooker-maximum-breaks\", \"score\": 0.6986712217330933, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: The Evolution of 147 Breaks in Snooker: From Rarity to Record-Breaking Feats\\nauthor: Author; Contributor Information\\ndate: 2023-08-17\\n---\\nEditor-In-Chief with 20 years experience covering the betting angles to breaking news stories. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored       "score": 0.5535233020782471,
      "text": "---\ntitle: 2022 Masters (snooker) - Wikipedia\ndate: 2021-04-15\n---\ntournament_name: 2022 Cazoo Masters\nlogo: 270px\nalt: Masters Snooker event promotional logo\ndates: 9 January 2022\nvenue: Alexandra Palace\nlocation: London\ncountry: England\norganisation: World Snooker Tour\nformat: Non-ranking event\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\nwinners_share: £250, 000\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\nscore: 10–4\nprevious: 2021\nnext: 2023\n\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\n\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. Daily slots player, Portsmouth fan and League Snooker Player\\n\\n25th April 2025 - Mark Allen hits latest 147 in defeat at the Crucible\\n\\nIn Snooker, achieving a 147 break is a rare and remarkable feat. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored the Last 147 in Snooker?\\n\\nWith Noppon Saengkham scoring the firs\", \"total_chars\": 48505, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.6863283514976501, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Masters_(snooker)\", \"score\": 0.6234679222106934, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: 2022 Masters (snooker) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2021-04-15\\n---\\ntournament_name: 2022 Cazoo Masters\\nlogo: 270px\\nalt: Masters Snooker event promotional logo\\ndates: 9 January 2022\\nvenue: Alexandra Palace\\nlocation: London\\ncountry: England\\norganisation: World Snooker Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. \"---\\ntitle: 2025 Championship League (invitational) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2024-12-17\\n---\\ntournament_name: 2025 BetVictorChampionship League Invitational\\nlogo: 300px\\ndates: 03 01 2025\\nvenue: bonuses)\\nhighest_break: (147), (147), (147)\\nwinner: Mark Selby ENG\\nrunner_up: Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. Three maximum breaks were made during the event, compiled by Jak Jones in Group 2, David Gilbert in Group 7 and Mark Selby in the Winners' Group; only the fourth time that three maximums had been made in a tournament, after the 2012 UK Championship, the 2017 German Masters, and the previo\", \"total_chars\": 18835, \"truncated\": true}]"

        "content": "[{\"docid\": \"60119\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Masters_(snooker)\", \"score\": 0.5535233020782471, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: 2022 Masters (snooker) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2021-04-15\\n---\\ntournament_name: 2022 Cazoo Masters\\nlogo: 270px\\nalt: Masters Snooker event promotional logo\\ndates: 9 January 2022\\nvenue: Alexandra Palace\\nlocation: London\\ncountry: England\\norganisation: World Snooker Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. Zhao Xintong, who entered the top 16 for the first time by winning the UK Champions\", \"total_chars\": 18181, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"1779\", \"url\": \"https://www.snookerisland.com/forum/viewtopic.php?f=25&t=11961\", \"score\": 0.5200641751289368, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Snooker Island's 50 Greatest Matches\\nauthor: Pink Ball »\\ndate: 2022-04-15\\n---\\nby Pink Ball » 15 Apr 2022 Read\\n\\nOkay folks, the votes are in, and I thank all 23 people who contributed to give us a fair idea of what Snooker Island thinks are the greatest snooker matches of all time. A total of 71 matches scored at least one point.\\n\\nHere's the schedule for tomorrow:\\n\\n1pm: I'll post the 'honourable mentions', a single list of all the matches that did not make it to the top 50, in order.\\n\\n4pm: I'll post, one by one, all the matches in the top 50 in ascending order, up to 21.\\n\\n5pm: 20 to six\\n\\n5.30pm: The number five\\n\\n6pm: The number four\\n\\n6.30pm: the number three\\n\\n7pm: the runner-up, immediately followed by the winner\\n\\nLast edited by\\n\\nPink Ball on 15 Apr 2022, edited 1 time in total.\\n\\n-\\n\\nPink Ball\\n\\n- Posts: 23089\\n\\n- Joined: 07 April 2015\\n\\n- Location: Galway city, Ireland\\n\\n- Snooker Idol: Those with nice faces\\n\\n- Walk-On: \\n\\nby gmg » 15 Apr 2022 Read\\n\\nWow, it took Chengdufan a full seven months to post his list and yet yours is gona take all of six hours! He is the most successful Asian player in the history of the sport. Throughout his career, he has won 15 major ranking titles, including three UK Championships (2005, 2009, 2019), and in 2014, became the first Asian world number one. He has twice reached the final of the Masters, winning once in 2011. In 2016, he became the first Asian player to reach the final of the World Championship.\\n\\nDing began playing snooker at age nine and rose to international prominence in 2002 after winning the Asian Under-21 Championship and the Asian Championship. At age 15, he became the youngest winner of the IBSF World Under-21 Championship. In 2003, Ding turned professional at the age of 16. His first major professional successes came in 2005 when he won the China Open and the UK Championship, becoming the first player from outside Great Britain and Ireland to win the title.\\n\", \"total_chars\": 75871, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"55516\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Selby\", \"score\": 0.5157273411750793, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Mark Selby - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2006-01-29\\n---\\nname: Mark Selby\\nhonorific_suffix: MBE\\nbirth_date: 19 June 1983\\nbirth_place: Leicester, England\\nprofessional: 1999–present\\nhigh ranking: 1 (Sep 2011–Nov 2012, Dec 2012–Feb 2013, Apr–Jun 2013, May–Jul 2014, Aug–Dec 2014, Feb 2015–Mar 2019, Aug–Oct 2021, Nov 2021–Apr 2022)\\nofficial maximums: 6\\nranking wins: 24\\nminor wins: 7\\nworld champ: 2014, 2016, 2017, 2021\\n\\nMark Anthony Selby (born 19 June 1983) is an English professional snooker player. Ranked world number one on multiple occasions, he has won a total of 24 ranking titles, placing him eighth on the all-time list of ranking tournament winners. He is a four-time World Snooker Champion, and has won the Masters three times and the UK Championship twice for a total of nine Triple Crown titles, putting him on a par with John Higgins, and behind only Ronnie O'Sullivan (23), Stephen Hendry (18) and Steve Davis (15).\\n\\nAfter winning the England Under-15 Championship in 1998, Selby turned professional in 1999, aged 16. He made his Crucible debut in 2005, and reached his first World Championship final in 2007, when he was runner-up to John Higgins. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify.       "text": "---\ntitle: Rob Andrew: it was like the Wild West when rugby union went professional\nauthor: Paul Rees\ndate: 2015-08-25\n---\nThe end of amateurism was like the fall of communism in eastern Europe: one day it was there and the next it had gone. The result of the chaos was that by 1998, the Rugby Football Union had lost £10.3m: although revenues had by then increased by 228%, thanks to a television deal with Sky, and costs went up by 309%, largely because of an increase in the number of staff. This article explores the world of maximum breaks, where players must pot all 15 reds followed by blacks and then the remaining six colours in sequence. We delve into the players who have made the most 147 breaks, with Ronnie O'Sullivan at the top of the list with 15. We also highlight the first-ever televised 147 break, accomplished by Steve Davis. Additionally, we examine the growth in frequency of 147 breaks since the 1980s and the fastest recorded maximum breaks. Get ready to be amazed by the skill and precision of these snooker legends!\\n\\nExploring the World of Maximum Breaks in Snooker!\\n\\nA volume that has seen 217 officially recognised 147 breaks recorded by 25th April 2025 as Mark Allen scored one at the World Championship, whilst being beaten by Chris Wakelin.\\n\\nThe first 147 was by the legendary Steve Davis. Coincidentally, Davis' was also the first 147 made in a televised match. Mark Selby's first ever World Championship final 147 in 2023 at the Crucible already seems a lifetime ago as the 189th.\\n\\nThere were earlier 147s\\n\\nMost Recent 147s - 2024/25 Season - Who Scored the Last 147 in Snooker?\\n\\nWith Noppon Saengkham scoring the firs\", \"total_chars\": 48505, \"truncated\": true}, {\"docid\": \"16091\", \"url\": \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_and_century_breaks_made_by_Ronnie_O%27Sullivan\", \"score\": 0.6863283514976501, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: Maximum and century breaks made by Ronnie O'Sullivan - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2018-12-17\\n---\\nRonnie O'Sullivan holds the record for the highest numbers of competitive centuries and maximum breaks, as well as the fastest maximum break of all time, in the professional sport of snooker.\\n\\nMaximum breaks\\n\\nRonnie O'Sullivan has made fifteen official maximum breaks in professional competition, the highest number completed by any player. This total only includes maximum breaks that have been ratified by the World Professional Billiards and Snooker Association (WPBSA); it does not include maximums compiled in exhibition matches, or in events that are not sanctioned by the world governing body.\\n\\nIn addition to the fifteen maximum breaks officially recognised by the WPBSA, other high-profile maximums include a 147 compiled against Joe Swail in the quarter-final of the 2007 Irish Masters. The maximum break prize of a Citroën Coupe, worth €20,000, was withdrawn when the organisers were unable to obtain insurance against a 147 being made. However, this was concealed from the players and O'Sullivan only learned of the withdrawal of the prize after he had made the maximum.\\n\\nAt the 2016 Welsh Open, O'Sullivan was on course for a 147 after potting 14 reds and 13 blacks, but then opted to pot the pink instead of the black, meaning he finished with a 146 break instead. He said this was in protest against what he believed to be the insufficient prize money awarded for the maximum, which in that tournament stood at £10,000 for the 147 in addition to the £2,000 highest break prize. \"https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Masters_(snooker)\", \"score\": 0.6234679222106934, \"text\": \"---\\ntitle: 2022 Masters (snooker) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2021-04-15\\n---\\ntournament_name: 2022 Cazoo Masters\\nlogo: 270px\\nalt: Masters Snooker event promotional logo\\ndates: 9 January 2022\\nvenue: Alexandra Palace\\nlocation: London\\ncountry: England\\norganisation: World Snooker Tour\\nformat: Non-ranking event\\ntotal prize fund: £725, 000\\nwinners_share: £250, 000\\nhighest_break: Stuart Bingham ENG (139)\\nwinner: Neil Robertson AUS\\nrunner_up: Barry Hawkins ENG\\nscore: 10–4\\nprevious: 2021\\nnext: 2023\\n\\nThe 2022 Masters (officially the 2022 Cazoo Masters) was a professional non-ranking snooker tournament that took place from 9 to 16 January 2022 at Alexandra Palace in London, England. It was the 48th staging of the Masters tournament, which was first held in 1975, and the second of three Triple Crown events in the 2021–22 snooker season, following the 2021 UK Championship and preceding the 2022 World Snooker Championship. Broadcast by the BBC and Eurosport in Europe, it was sponsored for the first time by car retailer Cazoo.\\n\\nThe participants were invited to the tournament based on the world rankings as they stood after the UK Championship. Some players took issue with the cut-off date, noting that the in-form Luca Brecel, who had entered the top 16 by winning the 2021 Scottish Open in December, did not qualify as the event took place after the UK Championship. Ding Junhui, who had made 15 consecutive Masters appearances between 2007 and 2021, fell out of the top 16 after the UK Championship and failed to qualify. Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. \"---\\ntitle: 2025 Championship League (invitational) - Wikipedia\\ndate: 2024-12-17\\n---\\ntournament_name: 2025 BetVictorChampionship League Invitational\\nlogo: 300px\\ndates: 03 01 2025\\nvenue: bonuses)\\nhighest_break: (147), (147), (147)\\nwinner: Mark Selby ENG\\nrunner_up: Kyren Wilson ENG\\nscore: 3–0\\nprevious: 2024 (ranking)← 2024 (invitational)\\nnext: 2025 (ranking)→ 2026 (invitational)\\n\\nThe 2025 Championship League Invitational (officially the 2025 BetVictor Championship League Snooker Invitational for sponsorship reasons) was a professional nonranking snooker tournament, which took place from 3 January 2025 to 5 February 2025 at the Leicester Arena in Leicester, England.\\n\\nMark Selby was the defending champion, having won the tournament for the first time after beating Joe O'Connor 31 in the final of the 2024 event. Selby successfully defended the title, defeating Kyren Wilson 30 in the final. There were 177 century breaks made during the tournament, a record for any professional event, of which Jak Jones compiled 32, breaking the record for most centuries in a single tournament previously held jointly by Kyren Wilson and Matthew Selt. Three maximum breaks were made during the event, compiled by Jak Jones in Group 2, David Gilbert in Group 7 and Mark Selby in the Winners' Group; only the fourth time that three maximums had been made in a tournament, after the 2012 UK Championship, the 2017 German Masters, and the previo\", \"total_chars\": 18835, \"truncated\": true}]"
